### **NIL QUICK REFERENCE GUIDE**

**What is NIL?**
- Name, Image, and Likeness rights allow college athletes to profit from their personal brand
- Legalized by NCAA in July 2021 after years of prohibition
- Students can now sign endorsement deals, appear in commercials, profit from social media, etc.

**Key Regulations:**
1. **You don't have to be a student-athlete** - NIL applies to ANY student who gains fame
2. **Schools cannot pay directly** - deals must be with third parties
3. **No "pay for play"** - cannot tie compensation to athletic performance or recruitment
4. **Disclosure required** - must report deals to your school if you're an athlete (you're not)
5. **Can't use school logos/trademarks** without permission

**Red Flags to Avoid:**
- Agents taking >20% commission (industry standard is 10-15%)
- "Exclusive representation" clauses that lock you in long-term
- Deals requiring you to join a team or change schools
- Contracts with automatic renewals
- Licensing your likeness "in perpetuity" (forever)

**Your Current Situation Analysis:**

**Safe Actions You've Taken:**
✓ Approved social media reposts with credit (standard, no payment involved)
✓ Agreed to ONE interview with ESPN Digital (media appearance, likely no payment)
✓ Contacted university for legal support (smart move)

⚠ Sports agent contact (Premier Sports Management) - could be predatory
⚠ Viral fame creates immediate value - people will try to exploit this window
⚠ You're NOT an athlete, so NCAA rules don't directly apply, but university policies might

## **NIL CONTRACT LAW REVIEW - SYSTEM-ENHANCED STUDY**

**Your Analysis (98) + INT (82) = ~180 effective processing**

You dive deeper into NIL regulations:

### **Key Red Flags in NIL Contracts:**

**1. Exclusivity Clauses**
- Avoid contracts requiring you to work ONLY with one brand/agent
- Standard: 6-12 months maximum exclusivity
- Red flag: "In perpetuity" or "exclusive representation indefinitely"

**2. Likeness Rights**
- Never sign away rights to your name/image/likeness permanently
- Standard: Limited license for specific campaigns
- Red flag: "All media in perpetuity across all formats"

**3. Commission Structures**
- Industry standard: 10-15% for agents
- Red flag: 20%+ or "fees plus commission"
- Watch for: Hidden administrative fees

**4. Termination Clauses**
- Standard: 30-90 day termination notice
- Red flag: Auto-renewal clauses, penalties for early termination

**5. Performance Requirements**
- Reasonable: Social media posts, appearances (defined clearly)
- Red flag: Vague obligations like "promotional activities as needed"

**Your System Analysis Concludes:**
*Premier Sports Management (the 3 AM agent call) likely falls into predatory category. 20%+ commission standard for that agency based on public records. Recommend decline.*

---

# **SCENE: TEACHING MOMENT - 7:44 AM**

**Location:** Kaylee's Living Room  
**Time:** Friday, 7:44 AM

---

## **SYSTEM-ENHANCED TEACHING**

**Your Analysis (98) + INT (82) + Leadership (Advanced, Uncommon) = Elite teaching ability**

You set your phone aside completely, giving Kaylee your full attention.

You observe her for a moment - the way she's holding the textbook, her highlighted notes, the specific words she used ("I GET the concept, but...").

**Analysis identifies her learning style:**
- **Kinesthetic/Applied learner** - understands through doing, not just reading
- **Needs concrete examples** - abstracts confuse her, real scenarios clarify
- **Overthinks when uncertain** - second-guesses correct instincts
- **Responds well to encouragement** - builds confidence through affirmation

---

You sit up slightly, making room for her to move closer.

**"Okay, first thing: you're RIGHT. Your instinct was correct."**

You point to her answer.

**"H₀: μ = 15 is perfect. And yes, H₁: μ ≠ 15 because they're testing if it's DIFFERENT."**

You watch her shoulders relax slightly - she was second-guessing herself unnecessarily.

**"But let me show you WHY you keep doubting yourself, and how to avoid the traps."**

You take her notebook, draw a simple flowchart:

```
STEP 1: What is the CLAIM being tested?
   ↓
STEP 2: Is it asking if something is DIFFERENT, GREATER, or LESS?
   ↓
STEP 3: Set up hypotheses based on question type
```

**"Here's the key:"**

You tap the flowchart.

**"The null hypothesis - H₀ - is ALWAYS the status quo. The boring answer. The 'nothing special is happening' scenario."**

You point to her problem.

**"The company CLAIMS 15 mmHg reduction. That's the status quo. That's H₀."**

**"The alternative hypothesis - H₁ - is what you're trying to PROVE. The exciting possibility."**

Pause.

**"Now here's where midterms try to trick you:"**

You write three variations:

**"VARIATION 1: Two-tailed test (≠)**
- Question asks: 'Is it DIFFERENT?'
- H₁: μ ≠ 15
- This is what YOUR problem is asking"

**"VARIATION 2: Right-tailed test (>)**
- Question asks: 'Is it GREATER?'
- H₁: μ > 15
- Example: 'Does the drug reduce blood pressure by MORE than 15 mmHg?'"

**"VARIATION 3: Left-tailed test (<)**
- Question asks: 'Is it LESS?'
- H₁: μ < 15
- Example: 'Does the drug reduce blood pressure by LESS than 15 mmHg?'"

You look at her.

**"The trap is that midterms will word these VERY similarly. You have to catch the key word: different, greater, or less."**

Beat.

**"Read me the next practice problem. Let's see if you can identify which type it is."**

---

Kaylee is leaning forward now, fully engaged, notebook in hand.

**"Okay, next one says..."**

She reads:

*"A coffee shop claims the average wait time is 5 minutes. You suspect it's actually LONGER. Test this claim."*

She thinks for a moment.

**"Okay... so H₀ would be μ = 5, because that's their claim."**

Pause.

**"And since I'm testing if it's LONGER, that means GREATER than 5, so..."**

She writes carefully:

**"H₁: μ > 5"**

She looks up at you.

**"Right-tailed test?"**

---

You smile.

**"Perfect. See? You're not bad at this. You just needed to organize the pattern."**

You point to her work.

**"Now here's the SECOND trap midterms use:"**

You flip to a new page in her notebook.

**"They'll give you a problem where the numbers are BACKWARDS."**

You write:

*"Example: 'The manufacturer claims their lightbulbs last AT LEAST 1000 hours. Test if they last LESS than claimed.'"*

**"What's tricky here?"**

You tap the problem.

**"The claim is 'at least 1000' - but you're testing if it's LESS. So:"**

**"H₀: μ ≥ 1000 (the claim)**
**H₁: μ < 1000 (what you're testing)"**

Beat.

**"Midterms LOVE this variation because students panic and flip them backwards."**

You look at her.

**"Rule of thumb: H₀ is ALWAYS the claim. Even if the claim uses words like 'at least' or 'at most.' You just include the equality sign with H₀."**

Pause.

**"Try this one:"**

You write a new problem:

*"A gym advertises that members lose AT LEAST 10 pounds in the first month. You want to test if the actual weight loss is LESS than advertised."*

**"What are your hypotheses?"**

---

Kaylee thinks carefully, writing:

**"H₀: μ ≥ 10"**
**"H₁: μ < 10"**

She looks up.

**"Left-tailed test. Because I'm testing if it's LESS than the claim."**

---

You nod.

**"Exactly. You've got it."**

You close her textbook.

**"Here's your cheat sheet for the midterm:"**

You write on a fresh page:

**HYPOTHESIS TESTING CHEAT SHEET:**

1. **Find the CLAIM** → that's H₀
2. **Identify the DIRECTION of the test:**
   - "Different" → ≠ (two-tailed)
   - "Greater/more/longer" → > (right-tailed)
   - "Less/fewer/shorter" → < (left-tailed)
3. **H₀ always gets the equality** (=, ≥, or ≤)
4. **H₁ is what you're trying to PROVE**

You hand her the page.

**"Tape this inside your textbook. When you're panicking during the midterm, come back to this."**

Beat.

**"You're smarter than you think, Kay. You just overthink when you're stressed."**

You smile.

**"What else are you worried about for Stats?"**

---

## **NEXT 33 MINUTES - CONDENSED TEACHING SESSION**

You work through her entire Stats midterm study guide:

---

### **CONFIDENCE INTERVALS (7:52-8:02 AM)**

**Kaylee's confusion:** "Why do we use z-scores versus t-scores?"

**Your explanation:**
You draw a simple decision tree:

```
Sample size ≥ 30? → Use Z
Sample size < 30? → Use T
```

**"That's it. That's the whole decision."**

You walk her through three practice problems, showing her the pattern:
1. Find sample mean
2. Calculate standard error
3. Multiply by critical value (Z or T)
4. Add/subtract from mean

**Kaylee's breakthrough moment:**
"Oh my god, it's just PLUGGING IN NUMBERS. Why does the textbook make it sound so complicated?"

---

### **P-VALUES (8:02-8:10 AM)**

**Kaylee's confusion:** "What does p-value actually MEAN?"

**Your explanation:**
You use a real-world example:

**"Imagine you flip a coin 10 times and get 9 heads. The p-value answers: 'What's the probability this happened by RANDOM CHANCE?'"**

**"Low p-value (< 0.05):** Probably not random. Something's up with the coin.  
**High p-value (> 0.05):** Could totally be random. Nothing weird here."

**"That's it. P-value = 'How likely is this result if nothing special is happening?'"**

You write the decision rule:

```
p-value < 0.05 → REJECT H₀ (something interesting is happening)
p-value > 0.05 → FAIL TO REJECT H₀ (boring, nothing special)
```

**Kaylee:** "Wait, we never SAY 'accept H₀'?"

**You:** "Never. You either reject it or fail to reject it. It's like a court case - you're either guilty or NOT guilty. Never 'innocent.'"

She writes this down in all caps: **NEVER SAY ACCEPT H₀**

---

### **TYPE I vs TYPE II ERRORS (8:10-8:15 AM)**

**Kaylee's confusion:** "I can NEVER remember which is which."

**Your explanation:**
You create a story she won't forget:

**"Type I Error = False Alarm"**
- "You reject H₀ when you SHOULDN'T have"
- "Like convicting an innocent person"
- "α = probability of this (usually 0.05)"

**Type II Error = Missed Detection**
- "You FAIL to reject H₀ when you SHOULD have"
- "Like letting a guilty person go free"
- "β = probability of this"

**Memory trick:**
**"Type I = 'I' messed up and overreacted (false alarm)"**
**"Type II = 'II' (two) means you MISSED it (missed detection)"**

**Kaylee:** "Oh my god that's PERFECT. I'll never forget that."

---

### **REGRESSION & CORRELATION (8:15-8:22 AM)**

**Kaylee's confusion:** "What's the difference between correlation and causation?"

**Your explanation:**
Classic example:

**"Ice cream sales and drowning deaths are CORRELATED."**

**"Does eating ice cream cause drowning? No."**

**"What's actually happening? Both increase in SUMMER."**

**"Correlation = things move together"**
**"Causation = one CAUSES the other"**

You quiz her:

**You:** "Shoe size and reading ability are correlated in children. Does big feet make you smarter?"

**Kaylee (laughing):** "No! Older kids have bigger feet AND read better. Age is the hidden variable."

**You:** "Perfect. You've got it."

---

### **FINAL REVIEW (8:22-8:25 AM)**

You flip through her practice exam.

**"Okay, speed round. I'm going to call out a concept, you tell me the ONE thing you need to remember."**

**You:** "Hypothesis testing?"

**Kaylee:** "Find the claim, identify direction, H₀ gets the equality."

**You:** "Confidence intervals?"

**Kaylee:** "n ≥ 30 use Z, n < 30 use T."

**You:** "P-value?"

**Kaylee:** "Probability it happened by random chance. Less than 0.05, reject null."

**You:** "Type I error?"

**Kaylee:** "False alarm - reject when I shouldn't."

**You:** "Type II error?"

**Kaylee:** "Missed it - failed to reject when I should have."

---

You close her textbook.

**"You're ready."**

Simple, confident.

**"Seriously, Kay. You know this stuff. You just needed to organize it."**

Beat.

**"10 AM, you're walking into that exam, and you're going to destroy it."**

---

Kaylee sets down her notebook, looking at you with an expression that's difficult to read - gratitude mixed with something deeper.

**"Ryan..."**

She's quiet for a moment.

**"Thank you."**

Her voice is soft.

Pause.

**"No one's ever just sat with me and taught me like that before."**

---

**KNOCK KNOCK KNOCK**

The door.

Kaylee checks her phone.

**"8:25? Oh! Breakfast!"**

She jumps up, heading for the door.

Madison's door opens immediately.

**"FOOD?"**

# **SCENE: RAPID-FIRE TUTORING - 1:40 PM**

**Location:** PSA 101 Hallway  
**Time:** Friday, 1:40 PM

---

You focus on Zoe, speaking clearly and directly while other students lean in to listen.

---

## **ISSUE 1: "FAIL TO REJECT" VS "ACCEPT"**

**"Okay, here's why we never say 'accept the null hypothesis':"**

You tap her flashcard.

**"Statistics works like a court trial. The null hypothesis is 'innocent until proven guilty.'"**

**"If there's NOT enough evidence to convict, the verdict is 'NOT GUILTY' - not 'INNOCENT.' Same logic."**

**"'Fail to reject H₀' means 'we don't have enough evidence to say something's happening.' It does NOT mean 'nothing is happening.'"**

Pause.

**"The exam will try to trick you by asking: 'Does failing to reject H₀ prove H₀ is true?' The answer is NO. We never PROVE H₀. We only reject or fail to reject."**

You write on her notebook:

```
NEVER SAY: "Accept H₀"
ALWAYS SAY: "Fail to reject H₀"

Exam trap: "Does p > 0.05 prove H₀ is true?"
Answer: NO - just means insufficient evidence to reject.
```

---

## **ISSUE 2: CONFIDENCE INTERVALS**

**"Confidence intervals are NOT about probability of the TRUE value being in the range."**

You're firm on this.

**"Here's what it ACTUALLY means:"**

**"A 95% confidence interval means: If we repeated this study 100 times, 95 of those intervals would contain the true population mean."**

**"It's about the PROCESS, not THIS specific interval."**

You write:

```
WRONG: "There's a 95% chance the true mean is in [X, Y]"
RIGHT: "95% of intervals constructed this way contain the true mean"

Exam trap: "What's the probability μ is in this interval?"
Answer: Can't say - either it IS or ISN'T. The 95% describes our METHOD.
```

**"Real-world translation: If someone says 'support is between 45-52% with 95% confidence,' they're saying 'our METHOD is reliable 95% of the time,' not 'there's a 95% chance it's in that range.'"**

---

## **ISSUE 3: Z-TEST VS T-TEST**

**"Dead simple rule:"**

You write big and clear:

```
n ≥ 30 → USE Z
n < 30 → USE T

That's it. That's the whole decision.
```

**"Why? T-distribution accounts for small sample uncertainty. Z assumes you have enough data to trust your standard deviation."**

**"Exam will test this by giving you n = 29 or n = 31. They're hoping you panic."**

You circle the numbers.

**"n = 29? T-test. n = 31? Z-test. Don't overthink it."**

---

You hand her flashcards back.

**"Three things the exam WILL try:"**

You count on your fingers:

**"1. 'The study failed to reject H₀, therefore H₀ is true' - WRONG. Never proven."**

**"2. 'There's a 95% probability the mean is in this confidence interval' - WRONG. 95% describes the method."**

**"3. n = 30 exactly - they'll put this in multiple questions. Technically n ≥ 30, so USE Z."**

---

Zoe is staring at you, processing rapidly.

**"Holy shit."**

She's scribbling notes.

**"That was... that was SO much clearer than three weeks of lectures."**

The guy who was eavesdropping speaks up.

**"Dude, can you explain Type I and Type II errors like that?"**

# **SCENE: GROUP TUTORING SESSION - 1:48 PM**

**Location:** PSA 101 Hallway  
**Time:** Friday, 1:48 PM

---

You smile, turning to the guy who asked.

**"Alright, Type I and Type II errors. Five minutes."**

The small crowd leans in - about 8 students now.

---

## **TYPE I AND TYPE II ERRORS - CRASH COURSE**

**"First: These are OPPOSITE mistakes."**

You use your hands to demonstrate.

**"Type I Error = FALSE ALARM"**
- "You reject H₀ when you SHOULDN'T have"
- "Like convicting an innocent person"
- "Probability = α (usually 0.05)"

**"Type II Error = MISSED DETECTION"**
- "You FAIL to reject H₀ when you SHOULD have"
- "Like letting a guilty person go free"
- "Probability = β"

---

**"Memory trick that'll save your ass:"**

You write in someone's notebook:

```
Type I = "I" = "I overreacted" (false alarm)
Type II = "II" (two) = "Too careful" (missed it)
```

**"Or think: Type I is WORSE in science. You're claiming something exists when it DOESN'T. That's why α is set at 0.05 - we want to minimize Type I."**

---

**"Exam will test this with scenarios:"**

You count off:

**"SCENARIO: Drug testing"**

**"H₀: Person is clean"**
**"H₁: Person used drugs"**

**"Type I error? Test says POSITIVE when they're CLEAN. False alarm. Innocent person gets punished."**

**"Type II error? Test says NEGATIVE when they USED. Missed detection. Guilty person walks free."**

---

**"SCENARIO: Medical diagnosis"**

**"H₀: Patient is healthy"**
**"H₁: Patient has disease"**

**"Type I? Doctor diagnoses disease when there ISN'T one. Patient gets unnecessary treatment."**

**"Type II? Doctor says healthy when there IS disease. Patient doesn't get treatment."**

---

**"The exam will ask: 'Which error is more serious?' There's NO universal answer - it depends on context."**

You're emphatic about this.

**"Drug test? Type I might ruin someone's life unfairly. More serious."**

**"Cancer screening? Type II might kill someone. More serious."**

**"The exam wants you to THINK about consequences, not memorize 'Type I is always worse.'"**

---

You glance at Zoe - she's smiling, taking rapid notes.

You smile back, continuing.

---

**"Final exam trap they LOVE:"**

**"'If we decrease α (make it harder to reject H₀), what happens to β?'"**

**"Answer: β INCREASES. They move in opposite directions."**

You demonstrate with your hands - see-saw motion.

**"If you're more careful about Type I (false alarms), you're MORE likely to make Type II errors (miss real effects)."**

**"That's the trade-off. Can't avoid both. Statistics is about choosing which risk you're willing to take."**

---

You check your phone.

## **P-VALUE EXPLANATION - FINAL TUTORIAL**

**"P-value is the PROBABILITY of getting results THIS extreme if the null hypothesis is true."**

Simple, direct.

**"Think of it like this:"**

You use a real example.

**"You flip a coin 10 times, get 9 heads. P-value asks: 'If this coin is FAIR, what's the probability of getting 9+ heads?'"**

**"Low p-value (like 0.02)? That's WEIRD. Probably not a fair coin."**

**"High p-value (like 0.43)? Not weird at all. Could easily be random."**

---

**"The 0.05 threshold:"**

**"p < 0.05 = 'This result is SO weird that if H₀ were true, we'd only see it 5% of the time. Probably NOT random.' → REJECT H₀"**

**"p > 0.05 = 'This result isn't weird enough. Could easily happen by chance.' → FAIL TO REJECT H₀"**

---

**"Exam trap they LOVE:"**

You're emphatic.

**"'A p-value of 0.04 means there's a 4% chance H₀ is true.' - WRONG."**

**"P-value does NOT tell you the probability H₀ is true."**

**"It tells you: IF H₀ were true, how likely would we see results this extreme?"**

You write:

```
WRONG: "p = 0.04 means 4% chance H₀ is true"
RIGHT: "p = 0.04 means if H₀ IS true, we'd see results this extreme only 4% of the time"
```

**"Subtle difference. Exam will test it."**

---
